 Unlimited Release

 Printed May 2026

Charge And Dynamic Current On Tubular Antennas For Various
 Drive Conditions
 Larry K. Warne, Plasma/EM Software Dept.
 Kenneth C. Chen and Marvin E. Morris, formerly with Sandia
 Sandia National Laboratories
                                  P. O. Box 5800
 Albuquerque, NM 87123
 May 5, 2026

 Abstract
 The mixed boundary value problem of a tubular conductor is solved using an approximate represen-
  tation of its Fourier coeﬃcients. A two term solution is derived, which represents the solution over an
  extremely broad range of aspect ratios. This representation is used to find the electrostatic solution and
  capacitance of a charged tube as well as the solution of a tube in a uniform field and its dipole moment.
  This second case is directly useful as a model for a monopole electric field probe. This approximation is
  a special case of a representation using a combination of Chebyshev and Legendre polynomials. Combin-
  ing the charged tube and tube in a uniform field allows the solution of voltage driven tubular antennas.
  Comparisons are made with numerical solutions using piecewise sinusoidal representations of the current.
  The results are also generalized to the dynamic case and up to and beyond the first resonance. Simple
  corrections for finite gap and delta gap drives to magnetic frill drives are examined using infinite tube
  integral transform representations. Corrections between magnetic frill drives and coaxial drives are also
  given. Approximate drive corrections using conformal mapping and an eﬀective radius are also discussed.
  Finally, this eﬃcient current representation is applied to the magnetic problem involving simple tubular
  solenoids.

1 INTRODUCTION
A method was introduced in a previous paper [1] for the approximate solution of static mixed boundary
value problems in cylindrical geometry with insulating cylindrical media. It is shown in this report that the
same approach holds for the problems involving cylindrical conductors in infinite media. The problem of a
charged finite length tube and a tube immersed in a uniform field are addressed first, followed by the case
where a voltage drive is applied. In addition to a least squares collocation technique which was employed
previously [1], here a Galerkin approach is also used, where the moments can be analytically evaluated.
 It is shown that the mixed boundary value problem of a charged perfectly conducting cylindrical tube
of finite length, as well as a tube in a uniform field, can be accurately solved by representing the charge
density as the sum of a constant and a term containing the edge singularities. This approach represents a
special case of a representation involving a combination of Chebyshev polynomials and Legendre polynomials
for the charge distribution (each of these forms a complete expansion if an infinite number of terms are
included);note that Chebyshev expansions have been used previously in tubular cylinder scattering [2], [3].
The preceding problems using Chebyshev-Legendre expansions are show to be accurately solved for a very
broad range of tube aspect ratios only using two terms in the solution. A Galerkin approach is set up and
in this truncated special case leads to a two by two system for the coeﬃcients. The integral moments in the
Galerkin solution are shown to be exactly evaluated in terms of generalized hypergeometric functions. The
power series expansions thus yield very accurate and convenient representations which are easily evaluated.
 A combination of the preceding constant voltage and uniform field drive representations also allow the
solution to the gap voltage driven tube. These moments in these static models can also generalized to
accurately treat the dynamic tube responses.
 Drive corrections are also examined by use of the integral transform solutions of the infinite tube. Cor-
rections are given relating the finite gap or delta function gap drives with the magnetic frill drive. A simple
correction formula is also given between the coaxial drive and the frill drive [4], [5]. In addition, we also
generalize an approximate eﬀective radius technique, used previously to treat zero length monopole antennas
[6]. This approximate technique allows two-dimensional conformal mapping solutions to be approximately
applied to the three-dimensional tube structures to correct from gap drives to coaxial drives.
 The receiving problem is treated using an application of reciprocity to the magnetic frill driven antenna.
The resulting short circuit current is shown to be an accurate approximation to the coax driven antenna.

2.3.2 Separation Of Oscillatory Parts

                                                 J0 (λ) = R (λ) cos λ + S (λ) sin λ                                           (60)

                                                 Y0 (λ) = R (λ) sin λ − S (λ) cos λ                                           (61)

where s = eiθ R and the magnitude is
 ¯ ¯
 ¯ 2s 2 ¯
                 ¯A Γ (−s) Γ (3/2 + s) Γ (1 + s) Γ (5/2 + s) ¯ ∼ A2R cos θ e3 2πR−4 e±πR sin θ
 ¯ 3
 Γ (3 + s) ¯

                                           ∼ e3 2πR−4 eR(±π sin θ+2 cos θ ln A)                               (244)
To definitively determine the phase of the minus sign we note that

                                                   arg (−s) = ±π + θ                                          (245)
If we take the + sign for 0 > θ > −π and the minus sign for 0 < θ < π then

                                       +π sin θ + 2 cos θ ln A , 0 > θ > −π                                   (246)

                                        −π sin θ + 2 cos θ ln A , 0 < θ < π                                   (247)
This choice places a branch of −s on the positive s axis, which seems reasonable. It also makes the first
term negative. If we impose ln A < 0, then for 0 < θ < π/2 these expressions are negative. If we impose
ln A > 0 then for π/2 < θ < π these expressions are negative. Note that in Stirling’s asymptotic formula for
Γ (z), to have |arg (z)| < π, with these definitions of the phase of the arguments −s, s + 1, s + 3/2, s + 1/2,
and s + 3, we are staying within this range. The exponential behavior for large R is therefore decaying on
the closing contours of the integration. Closing the contour on the left we have double poles at s = −1, −2,
a simple pole at s = −3/2, and double poles at s = −5/2 − n , n = 0, 1, ..., ∞. Then

 Z ∞
                                 A (λ) K1 (λa) I1 (λa) cos (λz) adλ = 1 , |z| < h                           (395)
 0
Thus (394) and (395) together constitute the dual integral equations of the mixed boundary value problem
associated with the tube in a transverse uniform field. Again taking the solution to have the approximate
two basis function form

                                              A (λ) ≈ Ac J0 (λh) + As j0 (λh)                               (396)
the vanishing of the charge density outside the interval of the tube (394) is satisfied and the constant potential
equation (395) can be enforced at

 Z ∞
                           [A0c J0 (u) + A0s j0 (u)] K0 (Au) I0 (Au) cos (Zu) du ≈ 1 , 0 < Z < 1
 0

where we have introduced the scaling Z = z/h, A = a/h, and u = λ/h as well as AAc = A0c and AAs = A0s .
We choose a set of match points Zm = m/M and match at m = 0, 1, ..., M to generate a matrix equation
 N

 n=0

In this case we have N = 1 (two unknowns) and we expect M >> 1 generating an overdetermined system
which we can solve in a least squares sense

                                                           AT A x = AT 1                                           (398)

 π
Noting the two equivalent radii choices for actual gaps g ↔ ∆/2 and g0 ↔ ∆0 /2 we see that this correction

with truncation distance g0
 2

 π
If we have the capacitance for a delta function gap, truncating the local charge at distance g0 , we can find
the capacitance for an actual gap ∆ by adding the capacitive correction (583).

                       C ≈ 2πaε0 (8.648148285) ≈ 0.35747078 pF , Nc = 0 , Ns = 3                       (585)

 π

 A subsection below uses charge pulse basis functions with M = 5000 and for a delta function gap with
g0 = 0.05a, a = 0.743 mm, and electrode length h = 4.325 mm (this is the total positive electrode length
in this case) gives C ≈ 0.31556 pF; the preceding result with the correction C − Cg0 ≈ 0.3209909 pF only
diﬀers from this by 1.72%.
 Even
 ¡ the simplest result
 ¢ with the least number of basis functions Nc = 0 = Ns yields C − Cg0 ≈
2πaε0 8.2893577 − 2 π2 ln 2 ≈ 0.30616 pF which diﬀers from the pulse result with M = 5000 by −2.98%.
This accurate agreement of the Chebyshev-Legendre basis having a small number of unknowns with the
more accurate pulse calculation illustrates the utility of the approximate Chebyshev-Legendre model even for
monopoles having relatively small aspect ratios h/a (for which the first and second order Hallen asymptotic
expansions below are not accurate).

Approximate treatments of a zero length monopole (at the end of a coax) capacitance [4] made use of a
two-dimensional problem with gap

                                                   g =b−a                                         (587)
where a was the inner cylinder radius and b was the outer coaxial radius. In order to return from the
two-dimensional geometry to the three-dimensional geometry the approximate radial field in the gap was
averaged
 Z b Z b

to define an eﬀective radius

                                             ρe = (b − a) / ln (b/a)                              (589)
This was used as the transverse length to multiply the per unit length parameters in the two-dimensional
conformal mapping problem [4].

 120

How do we choose the best estimate for g00 ? We note that the tube gap solution includes interior charge
which must be removed for it to represent the coaxial exterior drive. It appears that we can choose g00 to
reduce the interior charge of the tube to near zero (using the narrow gap formula for consistency with the
narrow gap formulas for the exterior charge); we will now see in some comparisons that this choice seems
to work for fifty ohm coaxial regions (we will see in a section below that this choice actually seems to work
for coaxial regions at or below fifty ohm characteristic impedances). A section below shows that for small
gaps 2∆ << a (for the delta function gap the truncation distance is taken as g00 /a << 1) we can write this
monopole interior capacitance as

                        ∆Ci / (4aε0 ) ≈ ln (a/g00 ) − γ 0 − 4/45 ≈ ln (2a/∆) − γ 0 − 4/45              (610)
This small gap correction vanishes for g00 ≈ 0.514a or approximately g00 ≈ 0.5a.

5.5.2 Comparison Values For Coaxial Gap Correction Example
In the sections below we discuss frill drives with the Chebyshev-Legendre basis as well as numerical solutions
based on pulse basis functions. The preceding approximate coaxial correction can be compared to these
values with dimensions h = 4.325 mm, a = 0.743 mm, and b = 1.73 mm.
 For the Chebyshev-Legendre basis with a magnetic frill drive the capacitance is found as

                      CF ≈ 2πaε0 (4.6508300839) ≈ 0.19224183 pF , Nc = 0 , Ns = 3                         (645)
An additive correction to go from the frill to the coaxial drive is discussed below. In this case the correction
is ∆CM ≈ −0.0072095 pF so that the coaxial driven capacitance is

                      CF + ∆CM ≈ 0.18503233 pF , Nc = 0 , Ns = 3 , h = 4.325 mm                           (646)
which is close to the preceding values.
 The numerical solution discussed below with N = 5000 pulses and a delta function drive gave

                        C ≈ 0.31556 pF , h = 4.325 mm , a = 0.743 mm , g0 = 0.05a                         (647)
A correction which must be subtracted, which takes this to a magnetic frill drive, is discussed below. In this
case this correction is ∆CF ≈ 0.1236815 pF so that the frill driven capacitance is

                                           C − ∆CF ≈ 0.191875 pF                                          (648)
Adding the preceding coaxial correction then gives

                                       C − ∆CF + ∆CM ≈ 0.18465 pF                                         (649)
Directly building in the magnetic frill drive as discussed below, the pulse basis function code with g0 = 0,
h = 4.325 mm, a = 0.743 mm and b = 1.73 mm and M = 5000 gave monopole frill capacitance

                                 CF = QF /V0 ≈ 0.19282 pF , h = 4.325 mm                                  (650)
so that the coaxial capacitance is

                                 CF + ∆CM ≈ 0.185587 pF , h = 4.325 mm                                    (651)
Both of these results are similar to the previous approximate results with the conformal mapping correction.

5.6 Pulse Basis Numerical Model
For comparison with the preceding tube solution using a Chebyshev-Legendre function basis here we give
results using a simple pulse basis function charge distribution along the tube. The potential is written as

5.7 Magnetic Frill Drive Scalar Potential

5.8 Magnetic Frill Drive Azimuthal Electric Vector Potential
We can also represent the magnetic frill field in terms of the electric vector potential. If we take the drive
to be a magnetic frill with field
 V0
                                         Eρ (ρ, +0) =               , a<ρ<b                                         (741)

and image it to generate a magnetic current density
 2V0
                                         Kmϕ (ρ) = −                , a<ρ<b                                         (742)

or magnetic volume current density
 2V0
                                      Jmϕ (ρ, z) = −              δ (z) , a < ρ < b                                 (743)

Then we take the electric displacement as

                                                 D = ε0 E = −∇ × Ae                                                 (744)
with

                                                       ∇ · Ae = 0                                                   (745)
and

                                                      ∇ × E = −J m                                                  (746)
Therefore using

                                          ∇ × ∇ × Ae = ∇ (∇ · Ae ) − ∇2 Ae                                          (747)
gives

                                                    ∇2 Ae = −ε0 J m                                                 (748)
and taking only the azimuthal component
 ¡ 2 ¢
                                              ∇ − 1/ρ2 Aeϕ = −ε0 Jmϕ                                                (749)
Thus we find

5.9 Fit For Frill To Coax Capacitive Correction Factor

driven antenna Kmϕ (ρ) = 2V [ρ ln (b/a)] , a < ρ < b shown in Figure 21 has been carried out [2], [3]. The
capacitive correction is written as [2], [3]

                                             ∆CM = 4πaε0 /Af (a/b)                                      (820)
where the negative constant Af is tabulated [2], [3] in Table 1.

6 INFINITE ANTENNA FEED CORRECTIONS

6.2.3 Interior Charge Correction
The interior monopole charge correction can be found as the diﬀerence

                                     ∆QiT = ∆CTi V0 = ∆QT − ∆QeT = (∆CT − ∆CTe ) V0                               (883)
From the preceding values we then find the interior monopole tube capacitive correction for the delta function
drive as

                                      ∆CTi (g0 ) / (4aε0 ) ∼ ln (a/g0 ) − γ 0 − 2 (1/9 − 1/15)

                                                ∼ ln (a/g0 ) − γ 0 − 4/45 , g0 /a << 1                            (884)
A comparison of the previous series values (845) with this small gap logarithmic result is given in Table 2.

6.4 Previous Average Radius Conformal Mapping Correction Compared To
 This More Rigorous Correction
We compare here this rigorous correction to the delta gap antenna capacitance to obtain the frill capacitance
and then the coaxial capacitance with the previous approximate conformal mapping correction using the
eﬀective gap radius. We also consider extending the conformal mapping approximation to other impedances
by modifying the truncation point choice g00 .

6.4.1 Rigorous Frill And Coax Corrections
Here we need to correct the delta function gap with the delta gap frill correction and then correct for the frill
to coax. We subtract the capacitive correction ∆CF from the delta function gap to arrive at the magnetic
frill drive capacitance where

               ∆QF (g0 ) = lim [QT (g0 , z0 ) − QF (g0 , z0 )] = 4aε0 V0 [2 ln (a/g0 ) + 2cF (b/a)]
 z0 →∞

 162

     = lim [QeT (g0 , z0 ) − QF (g0 , z0 )] + QiT (g0 ) = 4aε0 V0 [2 ln (a/g0 ) + ceF (b/a) + ci ] = V0 ∆CF (g0 )   (948)
 z0 →∞

so that

                     2 ln (a/g0 ) + ceF (b/a) + ci = ∆CF (g0 ) / (4aε0 ) = 2 ln (a/g0 ) + 2cF (b/a)                 (949)
with the fit for the exterior frill constant

                     ceF (b/a = u) ≈ 1.4 ln (u/u0 ) − 0.16 [1/ ln (u) − 1/ ln (u0 )] + 0.05 (u − u0 )               (950)
with

                                                  u0 = (b/a)0 ≈ 3.3659                                              (951)
and the interior capacitance is

                                  ∆CTi (g0 ) / (4aε0 ) ∼ ln (a/g0 ) + ci , g0 /a << 1                               (952)
with

                                                    ci = −γ 0 − 4/45                                                (953)
To go from the frill capacitance to the coaxial driven capacitance we must also add the correction ∆CM

                                              ∆CM / (4aε0 ) = π/Af (a/b)                                            (954)
where the accurate fit to the constant is
 π √
                               Af (x = a/b) ≈             xα {1 − α ln (x)} , α = 1/ 2                              (955)
 ln (π/4)

function pulse tube solution with N = 5000 pulse basis functions gave

   With a = 0.743 mm and h = 100 mm we have Ω ≈ 11.190753 and Ωc ≈ 7.8044588 and

                                                C ≈ 1.4760201 pF                                       (1291)
For g0 = 0.05a the tube driven by delta function gap minus the thin wire correction is

                           ∆CT (g0 ) / (4aε0 ) ∼ 2 {ln (a/g0 ) − γ 0 − 1/9} , g0 /a << 1               (1292)

                                             C + ∆CT ≈ 1.597458 pF                                     (1294)
The delta function gap with M = 5000 pulse basis functions gave

                           C ≈ 1.61577 pF , h = 100 mm , a = 0.743 mm, g0 = 0.05a                      (1295)
This shows that for larger aspect ratio tubes the preceding asymptotic form is quite accurate versus this
numerical value (−1.13% error).
   With a = 0.743 mm and h = 20 mm we have Ω ≈ 7.9718774 and Ωc ≈ 4.585583 and

                                               C ≈ 0.53850292 pF                                       (1296)

                                            C + ∆CT ≈ 0.65994087 pF                                    (1297)
The delta function gap with M = 5000 pulse basis functions gave

                           C ≈ 0.611238 pF , h = 20 mm , a = 0.743 mm, g0 = 0.05a                      (1298)
However, for smaller aspect ratio tubes the preceding asymptotic form is not as accurate versus this numerical
value (+7.97% error).

8 DYNAMIC TUBE
The dynamic tube can also be treated by use of the same type of Chebyshev-Legendre basis. The prior feed
corrections are also applicable here since the feed region is usually quasi-static. This problem is formulated
in terms of the magnetic vector potential.
 The drives considered here include delta gap drives as well as frill drives. We also include more standard
piecewise sinusoidal basis function results as a comparison for the dynamic case.

8.1 Dynamic Tube Representation
The dynamic case with electric current drive uses Maxwell’s equations

                                                  ∇ × E = iωB                                          (1299)

                                               ∇ × H = J − iωD                                         (1300)
and constitutive relations

                                                    B = µ0 H                                           (1301)

                                                    D = ε0 E                                           (1302)
We can take magnetic vector potential to represent the magnetic induction as

                                                   B =∇×A                                              (1303)
and electric field using the electric scalar potential as

                                                E = −∇φ + iωA                                          (1304)
From Maxwell’s equations

                         ∇ × ∇ × A = ∇ (∇ · A) − ∇2 A = µJ − iωµ0 ε0 (−∇φ + iωA)                       (1305)
and using the Lorentz gauge

                                                ∇ · A = iωµ0 ε0 φ                                      (1306)
gives
 i £ ¤
                                         E=          ∇ (∇ · A) + k2 A                                  (1307)
 ωµ0 ε0
where the vector potential satisfies
 ¡ 2 ¢
                                               ∇ + k 2 A = −µ0 J                                       (1308)
If we assume that there is only a single component in the axial direction which is independent of azimuth

current I

The idea here is again to put two electrodes centered at z = ± (h + ∆) each having length 2h shown in
Figure 14.
 We take the vector potential, which must be even in z because the axial current is even, to consist of
both the preceding charged and uniform field type terms at each of the two electrodes

 These approximate Chebyshev-Legendre Nc = 0 = Ns four basis function results (dots) are compared
to piecewise-sinusoidal basis function moment method tube results described in the subsection below (solid
curves) with N = 1000 basis functions along the monopole length in Figure 27 (the dimension h listed on
the graph is the total monopole arm length, whereas the notation 2h is used for the total monopole length
in the Chebyshev-Legendre basis set); the black and red solid curves are the delta function gap results with
admittance at g0 = 0.05a. The tan solid curves add the frill drive correction and the gray solid curves add the
coaxial drive correction to the piecewise sinusoidal admittance data (as we did for the previous approximate
model data). The accurate agreement of the Chebyshev-Legendre basis having the minimum number of
unknowns (dots) with this more accurate calculation (solid curves) illustrates the utility of the approximate
Chebyshev-Legendre model for the resonant monopole having relatively small aspect ratio h/a.

8.3 Dynamic Frill Drive
The dynamic magnetic frill drive can be represented in several ways which will be discussed here.

 At low frequencies we can compare this piecewise sinusoidal solution with the previous pulse basis function
results. Monopole height h = 4.325 mm (2h in the notation of the previous subsection on the Chebyshev-
Legendre basis) and delta gap drive with g0 = 0.05a and one thousand basis functions (N = 1000) along
the monopole length gave C ≈ 0.31552 pF versus the previous static pulse basis function solution with five
thousand basis functions C ≈ 0.31556 pF. Monopole height h = 10 mm (2h in the notation of the previous
subsection on the Chebyshev-Legendre basis) and delta gap drive with g0 = 0.05a gave C ≈ 0.4386 pF versus
the previous static pulse basis function solution with five thousand basis functions C ≈ 0.4388 pF. Monopole
height h = 20 mm (2h in the notation of the previous subsection on the Chebyshev-Legendre basis) and
delta gap drive with g0 = 0.05a gave C ≈ 0.6098 pF versus the previous static pulse basis function solution
with five thousand basis functions C ≈ 0.611238 pF. Monopole height h = 100 mm (2h in the notation of the
previous subsection on the Chebyshev-Legendre basis) and delta gap drive with g0 = 0.05a gave C ≈ 1.646
pF (with 1000 basis functions along the monopole length the charge beginning at g0 = 0.05a is probably
not well resolved) versus the previous static pulse basis function solution with five thousand basis functions
C ≈ 1.61577 pF.
 Now at the first resonance we find the admittances with this piecewise sinusoidal basis function solution.
The h = 4.325 mm monopole resonates near f = 15.4 GHz where the admittance at g0 = 0.05a is Y (g0 ) ≈
0.026395 − i0.012789 S and the frill driven admittance is YF ≈ 0.026395 − i0.00082145 S. The coaxial driven
admittance is Ycoax ≈ 0.026395 − i0.0001216 S. Note that the inverse of the real part equals 37.88 ohms.
 The h = 10 mm monopole resonates near f = 6.7 GHz where the admittance at g0 = 0.05a is Y (g0 ) ≈
0.0272706 − i0.0598876 S and the frill driven admittance is YF ≈ 0.0272706 − i0.000782118 S. The coaxial
driven admittance is Ycoax ≈ 0.0272706 − i0.00047764 S. Note that the inverse of the real part equals 36.67
ohms.
 The h = 20 mm monopole resonates near f = 3.4 GHz where the admittance at g0 = 0.05a is Y (g0 ) ≈
0.0278339 − i0.003746216 S and the frill driven admittance is YF ≈ 0.0278339 − i0.00110404 S. The coaxial
driven admittance is Ycoax ≈ 0.0278339 − i0.00094953 S. Note that the inverse of the real part equals 35.93
ohms.

from the delta gap drive, as well as the frill drive corrected admittance, and the coaxial drive corrected
admittance using the piecewise sinusoidal basis function solution with .

using the capacitive correction ∆CF . The gray curve is the imaginary part of the input admittance with
a coaxial drive computed using the capacitive correction ∆CM . The dashed gray curve is the direct real
admittance solution with magnetic frill drive. The dashed brown curve is the direct imaginary admittance
solution with magnetic frill drive. The dotted results are the Chebyshev-Legendre basis using Nc = 0 = Ns ,
in other words a total of only four basis functions, matching the axial magnetic vector potential on the
surface.

simple Chebyshev-Legendre basis results (black and gray solid dots), are very close to these more accurate
coax drive monopole results (large open circles).

8.5 Transmission Line Model
An approximate transmission line model for the dipole (right half of the model represents the monopole) is
shown in Figure 35 which has the diﬀerential relations
 dV
                                                     = −Ezinc − ZI                                           (1529)
 dz
and

 230

 dI
                                                       = −Y V                                        (1530)
 dz
where the impedance per unit length is

                                                 Z = Zi − iωL                                        (1531)
and the admittance per unit length is

                                                    Y = −iωC                                         (1532)
with inductance and capacitance per unit length

                                           L/µ0 = Ωc / (4π) = ε0 /C                                  (1533)
where the expansion parameter can be taken as

                                                  Ωc = Ω + Cc                                        (1534)
with fatness parameter

                                                Ω = 2 ln (2h/a)                                      (1535)
and constant

                                                Cc = − (1 + ln 2)                                    (1536)
This constant value gives first order accuracy in the center input admittance at low frequencies (versus the
choice Ωe = Ω + Ce , Ce = 2 (ln 2 − 7/3) with a similar numerical value, which gives first order accuracy in
the low frequency dipole moment). The internal impedance per unit length of a cylindrical conductor of
radius a and metal skin depth δ can be taken as

mm, Figure 38 has h = 20 mm, and Figure 39 has h = 100 mm. We see from these comparisons that the
approximate transmission line model is qualitatively useful in all these cases, but is also reasonably accurate
quantitatively for h ≥ 10 mm.

9 RECIPROCITY AND RECEIVING ANTENNA
Maxwell’s equations with magnetic and electric current sources are

                                    ∇ × E = −J m + iωB = −J m + iωµ0 H                                                  (1551)
and

                                         ∇ × H = J − iωD = J − iωε0 E                                                   (1552)
Taking two sets of sources and fields labeled as a and b and subtracting Maxwell’s equations and using a
vector identity thus give

 ³ ´
 ∇ · E a × H b = H b · ∇ × E a − E a · ∇ × H b = −H b · J am + iωµ0 H a · H b − E a · J b + iωε0 E a · E b (1553)

Integration over the volume V with surface area S and use of the divergence theorem gives
 I ³ ´ Z ³ ´
 E b × H a − E a × H b · ndS = E a · J b − H a · J bm − E b · J a + H b · J am dV (1554)
 V
 S

For the sources limited to a finite region we can take the integration range to become unbounded and drop
the surface integral to obtain
 Z ³ ´ Z ³ ´
 a b a b
                               E · J − H · J m dV =          E b · J a − H b · J am dV             (1555)
 V V

Figure 40 shows the three problems receiving (r), transmitting (t), and incident (i) for which we apply this
reciprocity relation in the next subsection [15].

9.2 Chebyshev-Legendre Basis Eﬀective Height
The leading terms of the Chebyshev-Legendre basis representations are used here with 2h replaced by 2h0
which is now taken to equal the total arm length h.

10 STATIC LOOP PROBLEM REPRESENTATIONS
The basis functions used on the tube can also be used for a conductive loop solenoid.

10.1 Loop Scalar Potential Representation
One approach to the problem is to use the magnetic scalar potential where the magnetic field intensity is

                                                          H = −∇φm                                            (1647)
with constitutive relation

                                                           B = µ0 H                                           (1648)
and the Gauss law (without magnetic charge)

                                                           ∇·B =0                                             (1649)
giving the Laplace equation (symmetric in ϕ)
 µ ¶

10.2 Loop Vector Potential Representation
Alternatively, we can use the magnetic vector potential with magnetic induction

                                                      B =∇×A                                             (1657)
and from Ampere’s law (in a region without electric current and at low frequency)

                                                      ∇×H =0                                             (1658)
Then using the identity

                                         ∇ × ∇ × A = ∇ (∇ · A) − ∇2 A                                    (1659)
and the Coulomb gauge

                                                         ∇·A=0                                           (1660)

                                                         ∇2 A = 0                                        (1661)
The component here is taken as the azimuthal component (symmetric in ϕ)

11 CONCLUSIONS
It is shown that the mixed boundary value problem of a charged perfectly conducting cylindrical tube of
finite length can be accurately solved by representing the charge density as the sum of a constant and a
term containing the edge singularities. This approach represents a special case of a representation involving
a combination of Chebyshev polynomials and Legendre polynomials (each of which can form a complete
expansion) for the charge distribution. The capacitance and charge distribution are found from this solution.
 This approach is also applied to problem of a tube in a uniform field. The dipole moments and center
currents are found from the solution. This problem solution applies directly to the problem of a monopole
electric field probe.
 The preceding problems are show to be accurately solved for a very broad range of tube aspect ratios
only using two terms in the solution. A Galerkin approach is set up and in this truncated special case leads
to a two by two system for the coeﬃcients. The integral moments in the Galerkin solution are shown to be
exactly evaluated in terms of generalized hypergeometric functions. The power series expansions thus yield
very accurate and convenient representations which are easily evaluated.
 A combination of the preceding constant voltage and field drive representations also allow the solution
to the gap voltage driven tube. These solutions again are shown to accurately represent the solution with a
minimal number of terms. Comparisons are made between this approach and the more standard numerical
approach using large numbers of charge pulse basis functions.
 Drive corrections are also examined by use of the integral transform solutions of the infinite tube. Cor-
rections are given relating the finite gap or delta function gap drives and the magnetic frill drive. A simple
correction formula is also given between the coaxial drive and the magnetic frill drive using previous model
data. Hence with these corrections coaxial driven tubes can be modeled using only the simpler delta function
gap solutions. We also generalize an approximate eﬀective radius technique used previously on zero length
monopole antennas to corrections for finite length monopole antennas. This approximate technique allows
two-dimensional conformal mapping solutions to be approximately applied to the three-dimensional tube
structures to correct from gap drives to coaxial drives.
 The previous static models are also generalized to treat the dynamic tube responses up through and
beyond the resonances. It is shown that the leading terms in the Chebyshev-Legendre basis accurately
model the dynamic tube through the first resonance. Eﬀective height responses are also discussed. It
is shown that the short circuit received current with frill drive also approximately represents the coaxial
drive. Comparisons are made between this approach and the more standard numerical approach using large
numbers of piecewise sinusoidal basis functions for the current with both delta function and magnetic frill
drives with corrections for coaxial drive conditions. Comparisons are also given with a complete coaxial
solution.
 The static models are also applied to perfectly conducting single turn current solenoids and compared to
uniformly distributed current solenoids. Inductance and central induction responses are compared.
